Yours is not finished, so anything we don’t like in it, we shall think is not going to be there. So it is easier for you.”

===Chinese===
「唉，人能做那种事吗？」邦珀斯说。「既然已经做了另一件。再说，那种东西来了又去，一旦上了纸，便自己存在了。不过我承认，我倒愿意能把它拿出来，领它的功。」

「上了纸便自己存在了！」艾米丽说。「它有那么好吗，是吗？那我愿你领得这份功。」

「也许真可以请谁来开始读它，」弗莱彻先生说。

「也许它自己就能读自己，」特蕾莎说。

「艾米丽，艾米丽！」赫里克说。「请原谅我这个妹妹，邦珀斯。她今天害得我全无心思读自己的书了。真是一点心思都没有了。我们就只听你的。无论如何，先听你的。你的还没写完，我们不喜欢的地方，都可以想成将来不会留在里面。所以你还轻松些。」

===Original===
“Why should the first be last, and the last first?” said Bumpus. “Mine will not bear hearing as yours will. My real book is where we know it is.”

“Why, yours will do as well, Dickie,” said Emily. “Much better, I am afraid. You can see that Nicholas is too frightened to read his, now that the moment comes. And perhaps it is a good sign. Good writers always feel a great wave of depression about their work: and Nicholas’ symptoms were so bad. Yours are all right now.”

“Well, well, then, I will start,” said Bumpus. “But I wish it were not this book that I was reading.”

“Richard,” said Masson, in a quick, expressionless voice, “you know that copy of your other book, that you gave to me once, when you thought it was too illegible to be used? I think now is the time to tell you that I kept it. I put it by, before﻿ ﻿… when there was no question of the book’s not coming out. And then I did not feel sure in taking on myself to destroy it. So I have it, if you can use it.”

“You have it? You kept the copy? That copy I gave you all those years ago, that I was going to burn? Oh, I remember now. You had not read it, had you? We were going to put it on the fire without your reading it?”

===Chinese===
「凭什么在前的要在后，在后的要在前？」邦珀斯说。「我那本不像你那本经得起听。我真正的书在哪儿，我们都知道。」

「哎呀，你那本也行，迪基，」艾米丽说。「恐怕还好得多。你瞧尼古拉斯，到了这关口，怕得读不出自己那本了。这或许还是个好兆头。好作家对自己的作品总要掀起一阵大大的消沉：尼古拉斯的症状就重得很。你此刻全好了。」

「好吧，好吧，那我就开始，」邦珀斯说。「只是我希望念的不是这一本。」

「理查德，」马森用一种急促而毫无表情的声音说，「你还记得你另一本书的那份稿子吗，有一回你给我的，那时你觉得它涂得太乱没法用了？我想现在是时候告诉你，我把它留下了。我把它收了起来，在……在还谈不上这本书出不出来的时候。后来我不敢擅自把它销毁。所以它在我这儿，你要是能用得上。」

「你留着它？你把那份稿子留下了？多年前我给你的那份，我原要烧掉的那份？哦，我想起来了。你当时没读过它吧？我们原打算不等你读就把它扔进火里？」

===Original===
“I did not read it then,” said Masson. “I seldom read any but scientific books, as you know, even my friends’ books. I did not even in those days. But when it was to go, I read it; to have my own impression of something of yours. I knew I had been at liberty to read it. And then I could not feel sure in destroying it. I should not have spoken of it, had you not expressed a wish for it, and I had provided for it to be burned at my death. I may seem to have taken much on myself. But I was helpless. I felt I could do nothing else.”

There was a silence.

“Oh, well, I will look over it again,” said Bumpus. “It may not be what I thought. But it is good news to me in a way. Thank you, William, thank you. I will try to use it, indeed. I will let this one go by for the time, and get the other out as soon as possible. After all, it is foolish not to change my mind when I have really changed it. And I have shown you all that I have done that.”

“Yes, even William,” said Emily. “And it is such an opportunity for being above self-consciousness and convention and other things. It would be dreadful to waste it.”

“Well, I will not waste it,” said Bumpus. “I would rather work on the other book. I couldn’t deal with the two at once. I dare say it isn’t as good as I thought. I was greatly younger then. But I don’t feel I could go against you, William. Or myself either. And I won’t read this one today. This book will go to the wall for some time to come. Who ever had so good a friend?”

===Chinese===
「我当时没有读，」马森说。「我很少读科学以外的书，这你知道，连朋友的书也是。那些年就更不读。可它要没了，我读了它；好对属于你的东西留下我自己的印象。我知道我原本是可以读它的。之后我不敢擅自销毁它。若不是你自己流露了想要它的意思，我不会提这件事，而且我已在遗嘱里安排我死后将它烧掉。我也许显得自作主张过了头。可我无能为力。我觉得舍此别无他法。」

一阵沉默。

「哦，好吧，我会把它再看一遍，」邦珀斯说。「它也许不是我以为的那样。但这对我来说某种意义上是好消息。谢谢你，威廉，谢谢你。我会想法子用它，真的。这一本我暂且搁下，尽快把那一本弄出来。说到底，既然我心意已改，再不改口就是愚蠢。而我已经让你们各位看到我改了。」

「是啊，连威廉也如此，」艾米丽说。「这可是个超脱于羞怯、俗套以及其余种种的大好机会。浪费了可就糟了。」

「唉，我不会浪费它，」邦珀斯说。「我宁愿做那一本。两本齐来我应付不了。我看它未必有我想的那么好。那时我年轻得多。可我不能违拗你，威廉。也不能违拗我自己。今天这本我就不念了。这本书往后有一阵子要靠边站了。谁有过这么好的朋友呢？」

===Original===
“And I won’t read mine,” said Herrick. “I too will wait for a future day. It wouldn’t be a good thing now. I shouldn’t take any interest in it, myself. My congratulations, my congratulations, Bumpus. I am glad, glad for you. I think you will always be the man of letters of us. I feel doubts about my own book after all. I feel a great wave of doubt about it. I believe I shall not have it finished for a spell of time, I believe I shall be like Crabbe, a man that all his friends think ought to write, and who never does. But I hope that like him, I shall encourage my friends.”

“No, no, no. What reason is there in this?” said Mr. Fletcher. “Why should we be baulked of the present books? You can read yours, surely, Richard, and then go on and finish it, before you get on to the other. And we must hear yours, Herrick; if not now, at some time very soon. Whatever has come to both of you?”

“I couldn’t read this one today,” said Bumpus. “William has put me off it, thrown me right back from it. I couldn’t get the other out of my head. I must go straight on to it. I am on it at this moment, in my mind. I shan’t be on this one again until the other is done. And it may have to be remodelled then, in the light of the old one. A man can’t do his own books entirely apart. I may have got some of the one into the other. The old one was so much a part of myself. But I dare say it isn’t so much good, really. I took things hard when I was young. Well, well, let us leave it all alone. How patient you all are! I must be getting away.”

“What insight we are getting into the minds of writers!” said Emily. “No wonder they are not much good at things that can’t so well be held up. Dickie and Nicholas have both proved that they are authors at heart. But what they are in any other way, we are not to know yet. Nicholas, you must work some more at your book, to make up for not having an extra one from your youth. It is such a pity you haven’t one. It makes you so inferior to Dickie now, and it left you so much time then for other things.”

“Oh, I think I shall leave my books to chance, and interest myself in Richard’s,” said Herrick.

===Chinese===
「我那本我也不念了，」赫里克说。「我也等来日吧。眼下念不合适。我自己都不会对它起一点兴致。恭喜你，恭喜你，邦珀斯。我为你高兴，真高兴。我想你永远是我们中间的文人。说到底，我对我自己的书起了疑心。一大阵疑心朝它涌来。我看这一阵子我是完不成它了，我看我会像克拉布一样，一个人人都说该写作、却从不写作的人。不过我希望像他一样，我也能鼓励我的朋友们。」

「不，不，不。这算什么道理？」弗莱彻先生说。「为什么眼面前的两部书我们一部也听不成？理查德，你完全可以念你那本，念完接着把它写完，再去做另一本。赫里克那本我们也非听不可；不趁现在，也该就在近日。你们两个到底怎么了？」

「今天我读不了这一本，」邦珀斯说。「威廉把我的心绪打断了，把我整个抛了回去。另一本在我脑子里赶不走。我必须径直扑到它上面去。此刻我的心思已经在它上面了。那一本不成，这一本我也回不来了。到那时候它也许还得照旧那一本的样子重新改构。一个人做自己的两本书，没法全然分开。这一本里说不定已经掺进了那一本的成分。旧的那本曾是我身上那么大的一部分。不过我看它实在也没有那么好。我年轻时把事情看得太重。好了，好了，这些都不提了。你们大家多耐心啊！我该走了。」

「我们对作家的头脑窥见得多深啊！」艾米丽说。「难怪他们不大擅长那些不好公之于众的事。迪基和尼古拉斯都已证明自己骨子里是作家。可他们在别的方面是什么样，我们还无从知道。尼古拉斯，你的书还得多下些功夫，补上你年轻时没有多出那一本的亏空。你没有一本真可惜。这使你现在比迪基矮了一截，当年倒给你省出那么多工夫去干别的。」

「哦，我想我的书就交给运气吧，我来关心理查德的，」赫里克说。

===Original===
“But are we not to have the books?” said Masson.

“You are not the one to complain,” said Emily. “You have had one of Dickie’s books to yourself, and no one else has had anything at all. And don’t be out of sympathy with the erraticisms of genius, because no one should be too far removed from it.”

“We are carrying it off very shamelessly,” said Bumpus.

“Are you?” said Emily. “That always seems to me the one thing authors are not, shameless. I think it would be better and safer for you to go, for fear we might bring it home to you. Peter and William were trying.”

“Two books in the hand, and now one in the bush,” said Theresa.

===Chinese===
「可我们两部书一部也听不上了吗？」马森说。

「抱怨轮不到你，」艾米丽说。「迪基的书你就独占过一本，旁人可什么都没捞着。再说，别对天才的任性失去同情，谁也不该离天才太远。」

「我们这样遮掩过去，脸皮未免太厚，」邦珀斯说。

「是吗？」艾米丽说。「依我看，不要脸恰恰是作家们唯一没有的品性。我看你们还是走了更妥当更安全，免得我们把这些坐实了给你们听。彼得和方才威廉就差点把话说破。」

「到手的两本书，如今成了一只在林子里的，」特蕾莎说。

===Original===
“Theresa, you are really the only genius here,” said Emily. “Genius is spontaneous, and the genius of Dickie and Nicholas doesn’t seem to be that. You had better go home, Nicholas dear, and leave Dickie and William on your way. You want a rest, and you can’t get that with greatness about. Dickie is great intellectually, and William morally. Moral greatness is the best, though I have always wondered if that was true. And I am going to talk to Theresa. Peter will walk with all of you, won’t you, Peter?”

“Isn’t it your birthday today, dear?” said Theresa, as the two women were left alone.

“Yes, so it is,” said Emily. “That is why I am out of spirits. I am fifty-one; and I don’t like getting to have so little life left. And yet I don’t much like living, which is absurd, and makes it impossible for things to be planned for me; because what can be done?”

“You don’t like living, dear?” said Theresa.

“No,” said Emily. “Of course I don’t like it. This has been the worst birthday of all the fifty.”

===Chinese===
「特蕾莎，这屋里真正的天才只有你，」艾米丽说。「天才是自发的，而迪基和尼古拉斯的天才似乎不是。你最好回家去，亲爱的尼古拉斯，顺路把迪基和威廉捎上。你需要休息，身边围着伟大，你休息不成。迪基伟大在智力上，威廉伟大在道德上。道德的伟大最好，虽说这一点我一向将信将疑。我留下来陪特蕾莎说话。彼得送你们各位，好不好，彼得？」

「今天是你的生日吧，亲爱的？」两个女人单独留下后，特蕾莎说。

「是啊，正是，」艾米丽说。「所以我才无精打采。我五十一了；一想到剩下的日子这么少，我就不痛快。可我又不怎么喜欢活着，这真是荒唐，倒叫人没法替我打算；因为还能怎么办呢？」

「你不喜欢活着，亲爱的？」特蕾莎说。

「不喜欢，」艾米丽说。「我当然不喜欢。这是五十个生日里最坏的一个。」

===Original===
“What is it? What is it all?” said Theresa. “I see something, and yet I don’t know quite what to see.”

“Oh, Theresa,” said Emily. “Did you see? Did you see? Nobody else saw? The men didn’t see? I always thought I had a man’s mind, but I must have a woman’s instinct after all. At any other time I should be ashamed of that.”

“You have nothing to be ashamed of, dear.”

“No, but Nicholas has not much,” said Emily. “Why shouldn’t he have a book, when Mr. Crabbe was dead and didn’t want it? Dickie found that a book wasn’t any good to a dead friend. After thirty years of thinking about it, he found that. And what was Dickie doing, leaving a book about, when Nicholas wanted one so much, and couldn’t make one for himself? And Dickie knew he couldn’t. He always unkindly never deceived Nicholas about it.”

“Mr. Crabbe?” said Theresa. “Mr. Crabbe? I only half understand yet. Was the book Mr. Crabbe’s? I thought it was Richard who left the book in the room? Oh, your brother thought the book was Mr. Crabbe’s? Oh, I see now. I thought he did not guess whose it was. I see now. I see.”

===Chinese===
「怎么回事？到底怎么回事？」特蕾莎说。「我看出点什么，却又看不清该看什么。」

「哦，特蕾莎，」艾米丽说。「你看出来了吗？你看出来了吗？别人谁都没看出来？男人们没看出来？我一向以为自己有男人的头脑，可到头来我必有女人的直觉。换任何别的时候，我都会为此害臊。」

「你没有可害臊的，亲爱的。」

「是啊，可尼古拉斯就没多少可害臊的了，」艾米丽说。「克拉布先生人都死了，用不着书了，他拿一本去有什么不可以？迪基花三十年想明白了，书对一个死了的朋友毫无用处。他想的正是这个。而尼古拉斯那么想要一本书，自己又造不出一本，迪基还把一本书到处乱放，这算什么？迪基明知他造不出。他一向不肯行个好骗骗尼古拉斯，从来不肯。」

「克拉布先生？」特蕾莎说。「克拉布先生？我才明白一半。那本书是克拉布先生的？我还以为是理查德把书落在屋里了？哦，你哥哥以为那本书是克拉布先生的？哦，我明白了。我原以为他没猜到那是谁的书。我明白了。明白了。」

===Original===
“Yes, that is it, I am sure,” said Emily. “But do you see the other thing? Did you see about Dickie? That book that he left in the room, that he had to write again, was the same as his early book, that was buried with his friend! I believe it was. I am certain it was. Did you feel that, when William and he were talking? He couldn’t begin his book, because William had read it. Of course I am not sure. But then I am. That is why Dickie is putting it away for the time. It is a good thing the book did not begin to read itself, as you said.”

“Oh!” said Theresa. “There was only one book, then? All three were the same book? Your brother’s and Richard’s, and the other of Richard’s that William kept? They were all just Richard’s old book?”

“Yes,” said Emily. “It sounds clever of Dickie, doesn’t it? You wouldn’t think one book would have to go so far. Dickie and Nicholas were both wonderful about managing things. You wouldn’t think they would find books so difficult. Poor Nicholas wanted a book so. And he knew Mr. Crabbe was dying and friendless, so that no one could have read his book. And it was typed, so that he couldn’t tell it by the handwriting. He thought it out so cleverly. Of course it makes it much worse of him. But perhaps he forgot all that. Criminals do forget something.”

“It was a shame!” said Theresa. “So you think that Richard remembered his book, and wrote it again? And then again, after your brother had﻿—had taken it?”

“Yes,” said Emily. “He had a lot of trouble, hadn’t he? But it was his own book. That seems quite good and strange of him. And worth the trouble, really. He was always saying how his book went back to his youth. He almost told us. He was really rather honest, considering everything. And William had read the book, which isn’t like William. That must have been put into William’s heart. Because of course Dickie wasn’t doing quite rightly.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，就是这么回事，我敢肯定，」艾米丽说。「可另一层你看出来了吗？迪基那层你看出来了吗？他落在屋里的那本，他不得不重写的那本，就是他早年那本，跟他的朋友一起下葬的那本！我相信是的。我敢肯定是。威廉和他说话时，你感觉到了吗？他读不成他这本书，因为威廉读过它。我当然没有十足把握。可我又确实有。迪基因此才把它暂且搁下。幸亏那本书没像你说的，自己开口读起自己来。」

「哦！」特蕾莎说。「这么说，书只有一本？三本全是同一本？你哥哥的那本，理查德的那本，还有威廉留下的理查德另一本？它们全都是理查德那本旧书？」

「是啊，」艾米丽说。「迪基这听着很聪明，是不是？想不到一本书能跑这么远。迪基和尼古拉斯操办事情都漂亮得很。想不到他们弄起书来这样为难。可怜的尼古拉斯多么想要一本书。他知道克拉布先生垂危，又无亲无故，谁也不会去读他的书。书又是打字的，笔迹无从辨认。他想得多周全。这当然使他的罪过重得多。可他也许把那些全忘了。罪犯总归会忘掉一点什么。」

「太不要脸了！」特蕾莎说。「这么说，你认为理查德记起了他的书，又把它写了出来？后来你哥哥——把它拿走之后，他又写了一遍？」

「是啊，」艾米丽说。「他吃了不少苦，是不是？可那本来就是他自己的书。他这样倒显得相当好，也相当奇。那些苦也确实值得吃。他总说他的书如何回溯到他的青春。他几乎都说给我们听了。通盘算下来，他实在算得上相当诚实。威廉读了那本书，这可不像威廉。那一定是有人放进威廉心里的。因为迪基那样做毕竟不大正当。」

===Original===
“Suppose any one of them had begun to read it,” said Theresa.

“Oh, I was afraid you would begin to suppose that! To read *it*! How dreadfully you realize the thing! And your sinister notion that the book might begin to read itself! I believe in religion now, and about our never being given to bear what is beyond our strength. Anyhow we shouldn’t be on our birthday. If it had happened, Nicholas and Dickie and William and I, and Peter﻿—but does Peter know?﻿—could never have met again. And we none of us know anyone else.”

“Peter does not know,” said Theresa, firmly. “What a thing for your brother that you have never married, dear! But I have often thought you would both be happier. Your brother married, as well.”

“Nicholas and I happier married! You don’t think, Theresa. Wives can’t think. Married people reveal all their past to each other, don’t they? Peter must have had a lovely past. Nicholas couldn’t do that. He hadn’t ever a good past, not at marriageable age. And he certainly couldn’t now. And he is seventy. He would have to marry an old lady. And she would not like the noise of the school. And it would really be taking advantage of Mr. Merry, because old ladies have so many little extra wants.”

“Does William suspect anything about the books?” said Theresa.

===Chinese===
「要是他们中间哪一个当真念了呢，」特蕾莎说。

「哦，我就怕你往这上头想！念*它*！你把这事体会得多可怕！还有你那个邪门的念头，说那本书没准会自己读起自己来！我如今信教了，信我们不会受领担不起的担子。反正不该在我们生日这天。真要发生了，尼古拉斯、迪基、威廉和我，还有彼得——彼得知道吗？——就再也没法相见了。而我们谁也不认识谁了。」

「彼得不知道，」特蕾莎斩钉截铁地说。「多亏你没出嫁，对你哥哥是天大的好事，亲爱的！可我常想，你们俩结了婚都会更快活。你哥哥也结过婚呢。」

「尼古拉斯和我结了婚更快活！你不动脑子，特蕾莎。妻子们不会动脑子。夫妻要把各自的过去全盘托出，不是吗？彼得想必有过一段可爱的过去。尼古拉斯做不到。他从没有过一段好过去，到了该婚娶的年纪也没有。如今更做不到。他都七十了。只能娶一位老太太。老太太又受不了学校的吵闹。那也实在占了梅里先生的便宜，老太太们零碎的额外需求太多了。」

「威廉疑心到书的事了吗？」特蕾莎说。

===Original===
“No, they none of them saw anything. Even Dickie and Nicholas each thought he was the only bad one. But they had enough to think of. So I know for certain that I could never marry William. For I find that I only like wickedness and penetration.”

“Shall you tell your brother that you know? Does he know you know?” said Theresa.

“I shall tell him nothing. If he thinks I know, or may know, we just shall not speak of it. Then that will be the same to him as my not knowing. Nicholas is like that. And what is adorable about him is, that he would think no less of Dickie. He never despises baseness. That is why it is so right of him to be base. I should appreciate him better, and owe him more, if I were more base.”

“No, it isn’t much good to you, dear.”

“No,” said Emily. “That is what I am saying. It hasn’t been quite fair to Nicholas.”

===Chinese===
「没有，他们谁也没看出什么。迪基和尼古拉斯各自都以为坏人是自己一个。可他们也够操心的了。所以我确切知道，我绝不能嫁威廉。因为我发现自己只喜欢恶和洞察。」

「你要告诉你哥哥你知道了吗？他知道你知道吗？」特蕾莎说。

「我什么也不会告诉他。他若以为我知道，或可能知道，我们便绝口不提。那对他而言，就同我不知道一样。尼古拉斯就是这样。他可爱的地方在于，他对迪基的看法绝不会因此打了折扣。他从不鄙视卑劣。所以他的卑劣才这样理直气壮。我要是更卑劣些，会更赏识他，欠他更多。」

「唉，这对你没多大用处，亲爱的。」

「是啊，」艾米丽说。「我说的就是这个。这事对尼古拉斯不大公平。」

===Original===
“Shall you tell him about Richard? Does he suspect?”

“I think he is not subtle enough, and was too absorbed in his own affairs. No wonder. Of course I shall not tell him. It is no business of mine. I am not even sure. I ought not to have said a word to you. I knew you couldn’t really know. They are none of them as base as me. I am quite a chance for Nicholas, if only he knew. But you won’t say a word, Theresa? Not a word.”

“Oh, no, dear,” said Theresa.

“I think I can trust you,” said Emily. “I know you don’t mind that kind of wickedness. So why should you reveal it?”

“What an extraordinary thing that they didn’t give themselves away!” said Theresa. “It all fell about in such a minute, too. It almost seems as if something must have been there to prevent it.”

===Chinese===
「理查德的事你要告诉他吗？他起过疑吗？」

「我想他不够精细，又太专注自己的事。这也难怪。我当然不会告诉他。这不关我的事。我连把握都没有。我对你说了一个字都不该。我原知道你不可能真明白。他们谁都没有我卑劣。我真该是尼古拉斯的一个良机，可惜他不知道。可你一个字也不会说出去的，特蕾莎？一个字也不说。」

「哦，不说，亲爱的，」特蕾莎说。

「我想我信得过你，」艾米丽说。「我知道你不介意那种恶。那你又何必揭穿它？」

「他们竟没露馅，真是奇事！」特蕾莎说。「这一切又全坍塌在一分钟之内。简直像冥冥中有什么在拦着。」

===Original===
“Hardly anything providential,” said Emily. “They couldn’t be thought to deserve that. But you and I certainly didn’t deserve its being spun out. Nicholas was afraid to read a thing that he couldn’t have written. Because of course he couldn’t have written Dickie’s book. He was always leading up to the shock of it. And it was natural for Dickie to tell us that he had had to rewrite his book. All that rewriting would have to make an impression. It was really unlucky that he hadn’t told us before. Things always happen so hardly on Nicholas.”

“But Richard might have begun to read his book,” said Theresa. “It was only William who by chance prevented him.”

“Yes,” said Emily. “If we are to persist in thinking unproved evil of Dickie, and of course we are to do that. That must have been providential, and I told you I had got religion from it. So organic too, for William to prevent it, when it was only he, who made the prevention necessary! William couldn’t have been so egotistic by himself. This may have happened to convince you how far he and I are from each other. Providential things seem to be circuitous, like that.”

“I can’t think why Richard couldn’t have said that his book was the old one,” said Theresa.

“When one book was so much above the average, too,” said Emily. “But he didn’t know it was. He thought it was being just equal to Nicholas, and that has never done for Dickie. Did you see the shock he had in thinking one sentence was the same? I wish my shock had only been that. And then there was going back on what he had done. That was the trouble. Knowing William would make that very difficult.”

===Chinese===
「算不上什么天意，」艾米丽说。「他们不配得这个。可这局被拉长，你我更不配。尼古拉斯不敢念一件他自己写不出来的东西。因为他当然写不出迪基的书。他一直在为那一惊做铺垫。迪基则很自然地告诉我们，他不得不重写他的书。那样一番重写，不能不给人印象。他以前没说过，实在倒霉。事情落在尼古拉斯头上，总是这么别扭。」

「可理查德本来已经开始要念他的书了，」特蕾莎说。「只是威廉碰巧拦住了他。」

「是啊，」艾米丽说。「要是我们坚持往没证实过的坏处想迪基——我们当然要坚持。那这必是天意了，我说过，我由此得了宗教。而且安排得多有机巧：让威廉去拦，而使拦阻成为必要的又正是他！威廉独自一人绝对利己不到这一步。这事的用意，恐怕是让你看看他和我相隔多远。天意的事，看来就是这样迂回。」

「我不明白，理查德为什么不能直说他的书就是旧的那本，」特蕾莎说。

「何况那本书高出寻常水准这么多，」艾米丽说。「可他自己不知道。他以为那不过跟尼古拉斯打了个平手，而打平手从来不能满足迪基。他以为有一个句子相同时所受的那一惊，你看见了吗？我受的惊要是只有那么大就好了。还有，把自己做过的事推倒，那才是难处。威廉知情，这事就更难了。」

===Original===
“I don’t know why he should want two kinds of credit,” said Theresa. “Not Richard.”

“That is beautiful of you towards him,” said Emily. “Why shouldn’t he want two kinds of credit? You wouldn’t ask that if you lived with Nicholas. Living with Peter must be so ennobling. There are Lydia and Peter coming up the road. It is nice to see Peter in innocent company again. I can’t meet them, Theresa. I am going home to write it all in my diary. I keep a diary, because I think I have that kind of personality. I must put in my will that it is to be destroyed at my death. For fear somebody should read it, and publish it, and pretend they had written it. Unless I leave it to Nicholas, so that he can have written a book after all. I hope he will outlive me. I would commit suicide, except that now I believe in religion, and religion does not allow that. And I am not single for the sake of Nicholas. I read in a book that no woman could love a man she did not make sacrifices for. But there is so much falseness about books. Too much, I think.”

“Emily is worth a thousand Lydias,” said Theresa, as her husband entered the study alone.

“Oh, you are too wise to talk of some people in terms of others, my dear. Emily is rare, of course.”

“Oh, you see that, do you, Peter?”

===Chinese===
「我不明白他要两种名声做什么，」特蕾莎说。「理查德不是这样的人。」

「你待他这样真是美，」艾米丽说。「他为什么不该要两种名声？跟你说的这个一起过日子，你就不会问这话了。跟彼得一起过日子一定极其高尚化人。莉迪亚和彼得从路上过来了。又见彼得结伴于清白无辜的人，真好。我不能见他们，特蕾莎。我要回家把这些全写进日记。我记日记，因为我自认是那一类性格。我必须在遗嘱里写明，我死后将它销毁。以防有人读了它，拿去发表，还冒称是自己写的。除非我把它留给尼古拉斯，好歹让他也写过一本书。我希望他比我活得长。我本想自杀，只是如今我信了教，教不许人自杀。还有，我不是为了尼古拉斯才独身的。我在一本书里读到，女人不可能爱一个她不曾为之牺牲的男人。可书里的假话太多。太多了，我看。」

「艾米丽抵得上一千个莉迪亚，」丈夫独自走进书房时，特蕾莎说。

「哦，亲爱的，你太明智了，不会拿这个人和那个人作比。艾米丽自然是难得的。」

「哦，这个你也看出来了，是吗，彼得？」

===Original===
“Yes, I see that, my dear. I see.”

Theresa looked at her husband, and did not speak.

Emily went back to her brother, and found him sitting by his fire, dreamy and unoccupied.

“Well, darling,” she said. “So you have given up your book! Have you done away with it?”

“Yes, I have destroyed it,” said Herrick, smiling at her. “Destroyed it, my Emily. I don’t like books, and that is the truth. I am quite put off them. Do you know, dear old Bumpus made a confession to Masson and me? He confessed that he had remembered his old book, called it up to his mind again, the one that was supposed to be in his friend’s grave, that is actually there; and made out that it was this new one. This one he was to have read tonight. The one that was destroyed in old Crabbe’s room. And to own that up before Masson and Fletcher! I couldn’t have done it, Emily. And I don’t think it is incumbent upon a man to keep nothing of his secret doings to himself.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，我看出来了，亲爱的。我看出来了。」

特蕾莎望着丈夫，没有说话。

艾米丽回到兄长那里，见他坐在炉火旁，恍恍惚惚，无所事事。

「哎，亲爱的，」她说。「你把你的书放弃了！你把它处理掉了？」

「是啊，我把它毁了，」赫里克说着，朝她微笑。「毁了它，我的艾米丽。我不喜欢书，这是实话。我彻底对书倒了胃口。你知道吗，亲爱的老邦珀斯向马森和我忏悔了？他招认说，他一直记得他那本旧书，又把它召回心里，就是那本据说葬在他朋友坟里、也确实葬在那儿的那本；还把它说成是这本新书。就是他今晚原要念的这一本。在老克拉布屋里被清掉的那一本。当着马森和弗莱彻的面认下这一切！我做不到，艾米丽。我也不认为，一个人就该把自己隐秘的行事一星半点也不留。」

===Original===
“Neither do I,” said Emily. “We should be afraid of having anybody talk to us. And we certainly couldn’t talk to anybody. I don’t mean it wasn’t wonderful of Dickie. But what he had done wasn’t enough to be a tax on anybody. For his very worst.”

“No,” said Herrick. “There was no great harm in it. But I confess I don’t readily follow a stretch of doubleness like that. A sudden temptation and yielding to it! That I understand. I think the highest type of person might be prone to it.”

“Everyone can understand that,” said Emily. “And the lowest type of person would be even more prone to it. But everything else just follows from it. That does want a little more cleverness to understand, or else experience. But I am sure you understand it, darling. Did William and Peter say anything?”

“They neither of them said a word,” said Herrick. “But they will not in the future. It is all over for Bumpus. Well, I don’t like books, to bring old Bumpus to that. Dear old Bumpus! It was a fine thing of him, when he was a young man, and a fine thing of him now to tell us of it. He told us to tell you, Emily. He remembered your making the suggestion that he should rewrite the book. It was not easy to him, I think, to have you told. I think he found me the easiest. And I confess I like to think that.”

===Chinese===
「我也不认为，」艾米丽说。「否则我们就怕听任何人说话了。我们也实在没法跟任何人说话了。我不是说迪基那样不了不起。只是他做的那些，还够不上让任何人受累。就算把他往最坏里算。」

「是啊，」赫里克说。「那里面没多大害处。可我承认，那样一长串的两面手法，我不容易跟得上。一时的诱惑加屈服！那个我懂。我想最高一类的人也难免如此。」

「谁都懂那个，」艾米丽说。「最低一类的人还更免不了。可后面接踵而来的一切，就要多几分聪明，或者几分阅历，才看得懂了。可我相信你看得懂，亲爱的。威廉和彼得说什么了吗？」

「他们俩谁也没说一个字，」赫里克说。「往后也不会说了。对邦珀斯而言全过去了。唉，我不喜欢书，竟把老邦珀斯弄到这个地步。亲爱的老邦珀斯！他年轻时那件事做得漂亮，如今告诉我们这件事，也做得漂亮。他特意嘱咐我们告诉你，艾米丽。他记得是你提的建议，让他重写那本书。我想，让你知道这件事，对他并不轻松。我猜他觉得我还是最好开口的。我承认，想到这一点我很受用。」

===Original===
“So should I,” said Emily. “You were rather unkind about me. I suppose I have proved that I should have done the same in Dickie’s place. I don’t know why he should find me difficult.”

“Dear old Bumpus!” said Herrick. “My dear old gifted, erring friend! Well, well, we all err. And this kind of thing, this literary ambition, is the thing that most of all leads men to error, I think. Do you know, Emily, I think that the best achievement of a man, the highest and largest thing, is to feel tolerance and generous love for a man who can do what is denied to himself to do. I do indeed. As I feel for Bumpus tonight. As I feel for my friend. I do indeed feel it, Emily.”

“Nicholas, you really are a genius,” said Emily.

===Chinese===
「换成我也受用，」艾米丽说。「你从前对我不大厚道。我想我已经证明，我处在迪基的位置也会做同样的事。我不明白他为什么觉得我难对付。」

「亲爱的老邦珀斯！」赫里克说。「我亲爱的有天赋、也会犯错的老朋友！唉，唉，我们都会犯错。而这种东西，这种文学的野心，我想正是最最容易把人引向过失的东西。你知道吗，艾米丽，我想一个人最好的成就，最高、最大的事，就是对一个做成了他自己做不成的事的人，怀有宽容和慷慨的爱。千真万确。就像我今晚对邦珀斯的感觉。就像我对我的朋友的感觉。我真真切切感觉到了，艾米丽。」

「尼古拉斯，你真是个天才，」艾米丽说。

## VII / 七

===Original===
“Miss Basden, just come and put a stop to all this, will you? Here is Mother, getting all into a state, with nobody thought of coming! I wish this dinner-party was at the bottom of the sea. Just see that she gets a breathing space, Miss Basden, will you?”

“Yes, I will, Mr. Merry. I have got fairly tidy myself betimes, on purpose to prevent such naughtiness,” said Miss Basden, who wore a remote expression, for which her toilet was responsible.

“Yes, Miss Basden, how we depend on you! And what a thing, all of this! All this fuss and change, and nobody coming who can do any good to the school! All this pretending that we do not live as we do, but in different rooms, and in a different way, as if the ordinary way did not make work enough! A second rate kind of thing, I call it, for all of us to be doing. And it isn’t as if Miss Herrick will not give us away, so that all of it is as good as nothing﻿—Ah, Miss Herrick! Ah, I did not see you, Miss Herrick. We were just saying how you would give us away, you know, and talk as you will to us, so that our guests would see through all our little changes. Ah, Miss Herrick, you and I both have our way of talking, haven’t we?”

“Yes, I am sure we have everything due to us. And it is so suitable for you not to like the second rate. But I am afraid the basement dining-room is that. I believe Mr. Bentley would think so. We ought to be going to the study, which is the drawing-room tonight. We have honestly left that as it is.”

Miss Basden hurried across the hall to open the door for Emily, and Mr. Merry followed as if too cast down to take this natural office upon himself.

===Chinese===
「巴斯登小姐，快来把这局面止住吧，行吗？妈妈这儿急得没了主张，谁都没想起来请！真希望这场晚宴沉到海底去。务必让她喘口气，巴斯登小姐，好吗？」

「好，我会的，梅里先生。我自己也提早拾掇停当了，就为防这种失礼，」巴斯登小姐说，她带着一副疏远的神情——那得归功于她的盛装。

「是啊，巴斯登小姐，我们多仰仗您！这一切又是何苦！这么一番折腾和改换，来的却没有一个对学校有半点好处的人！装得好似我们不住在原来那些屋子、不照原来的过法，倒仿佛平日那套还不够忙似的！我说，我们大家干的这是二流的事。再说赫里克小姐反正会把我们捅出去，这么一来全都白搭——啊，赫里克小姐！啊，我没瞧见您，赫里克小姐。我们正说您会怎么把我们捅出去呢，您知道的，说您会照您那套对我们说话，好叫客人们把我们这点小改动全看穿。啊，赫里克小姐，您跟我都有各自的一套说法，是不是？」

「是啊，我敢说咱们各有各的报偿。您看不上二流的东西，这也正合身份。只怕地下层饭厅就是二流。我相信本特利先生也会这么想。我们该去书房了，今晚它权当客厅。那一间我们可是实实在在没动。」

巴斯登小姐疾步穿过门厅去给艾米丽开门，梅里先生跟在后面，仿佛沮丧得连这个本属他的差事都无心承担。

===Original===
“What a lovely family group!” said Emily, as the Fletchers came in. “An uncle and a nephew, and a brother and a sister and an aunt, and a husband and a wife, and I think some more, all in four people! I wish Nicholas and I were something to Mr. and Mrs. Merry and Miss Basden.”

“Ah, you are, Miss Herrick, you are,” said Mr. Merry.

“And am I to be left out? That is very lonely for me,” said Mr. Burgess.

“Yes, yes, Mr. Burgess,” said Mr. Merry. “Why, Mr. Bentley! How are you, Mr. Bentley? Why, how nice it is to have one of the fathers of our laddies here among us, like this!”

“Let us go up to the fire, Miss Bentley,” said Mrs. Merry, “and leave the men to talk about the newspapers in the cold.”

===Chinese===
「多可爱的全家福！」弗莱彻一家进来时，艾米丽说。「一个伯伯和一个侄子，一个哥哥和一个妹妹和一个姑妈，一个丈夫和一个妻子，恐怕还有几重，全在四个人身上！但愿尼古拉斯和我也算得上梅里先生、梅里太太和巴斯登小姐的什么人。」

「啊，算得上，赫里克小姐，算得上，」梅里先生说。

「就把我除外吗？那我可太孤单了，」伯吉斯先生说。

「是啊，是啊，伯吉斯先生，」梅里先生说。「哎呀，本特利先生！您好吗，本特利先生？哎呀，我们小家伙们的父亲有一位这样光临，多好啊！」

「我们到炉火边去吧，本特利小姐，」梅里太太说，「把男人们留在冷处谈报纸。」

===Original===
“Why, what a way for your wife to talk in your presence, Mr. Merry!” said Delia.

“I don’t suppose wives ought to talk at all in their husbands’ presence,” said Herrick.

“Civilized countries are so artificial,” said Emily. “But you should not speak true words in jest, Nicholas. It is not open of you.”

“Well, what about us single women, Miss Herrick?” said Miss Basden.

“Well, I don’t suppose we ought to talk at all. I expect we ought to be exposed at birth, or something like that.”

===Chinese===
「哎呀，梅里先生，您太太当着您的面这样说话！」迪莉娅说。

「我以为妻子根本就不该当着丈夫的面说话，」赫里克说。

「文明国家就是这么矫揉造作，」艾米丽说。「可你不该拿正经话开玩笑，尼古拉斯。这可不是光明磊落。」

「那我们独身女人呢，赫里克小姐？」巴斯登小姐说。

「唉，我看我们更是一个字也不该说。我看我们生下来就该被扔掉，或者这一类。」

===Original===
“How would it be known at birth which of us were going to be single?” said Delia.

“That is really clever of you,” said Emily. “Though people exposed at birth would be single, wouldn’t they?”

“Well, we were certainly classed by the state with paupers and idiots and children, before we had the vote,” said Miss Basden. “I mean we women were.”

“And no nice children, or paupers either, and no really sensible idiots, would talk in people’s presence,” said Bumpus.

“Do we go in to dinner now, and pair off like the ark?” said Emily. “I think that is so useful. How clever of you, Nicholas, to have told everybody to take in everybody else, without telling them anything! But then men are clever.”

===Chinese===
「生下来怎么知道我们哪个将来会独身呢？」迪莉娅说。

「你这话真机灵，」艾米丽说。「虽说一出生就被扔掉的人，可不就都独身了吗？」

「反正拿到选举权之前，国家是把我们跟贫民、白痴和孩子归为一类的，」巴斯登小姐说。「我是说我们女人。」

「而乖孩子、正经贫民，还有真正明白事理的白痴，谁也不会当着人说话，」邦珀斯说。

「现在就入席了么，像诺亚方舟一样成双成对？」艾米丽说。「这法子真管用。你多聪明啊，尼古拉斯，吩咐每个人挽着别的人入席，却什么都不明说！男人到底是聪明。」

===Original===
“Do you really think, Miss Herrick,” said Francis, as the seats were taken, “that men are clever in such little ways, compared to ladies? I think many people would grant the ladies the palm.”

“I won’t answer for my sister, Fletcher,” said Herrick. “She will be leading you into danger. You would soon find yourself in her power.”

“I should never be anything but glad, Mr. Herrick,” said Francis, leaning forward, “to find myself in any lady’s power.”

“That is rather a rash statement,” said Mr. Burgess. “I haven’t lived so long as many of the people here, but I should hardly say that.”

“Ah, Mr. Burgess, so you have seen life, have you?” said Mr. Merry.

===Chinese===
「赫里克小姐，您真觉得，」落座时弗朗西斯说，「男人在这些小地方比女士们聪明？我想许多人会把棕榈枝判给女士们。」

「我不替我妹妹作保，弗莱彻，」赫里克说。「她会把你引入险境。不出多时你就会发现自己落进她的掌心。」

「落进任何一位女士的掌心，我都只有欢喜，赫里克先生，」弗朗西斯欠身向前说。

「这话未免轻率，」伯吉斯先生说。「我不如在座许多人活得久，可这样的话我说不出口。」

「啊，伯吉斯先生，这么说您见识过生活了？」梅里先生说。

===Original===
“I have seen life, Merry,” said Bumpus. “Do please believe me.”

“We won’t talk about that, Richard,” said Herrick.

“Nicholas is going to be a bright host!” said Emily. “Isn’t he wonderful?”

“How are your little fellows tonight, Miss Bentley?” said Mr. Merry. “They are growing, you know. We can’t stop that; and we don’t want to; but we have to keep an eye on them.”

“Francis, listen,” said Bumpus. “You are the only possible future parent in the room. Bentley’s boys are here.”

===Chinese===
「我确实见识过生活，梅里，」邦珀斯说。「千万相信我。」

「那个我们不谈，理查德，」赫里克说。

「尼古拉斯要当一位兴致勃勃的主人啦！」艾米丽说。「他是不是了不起？」

「您那两个小家伙今晚好吗，本特利小姐？」梅里先生说。「他们在长个儿，您知道的。这拦不住；我们也不想拦；但总得盯着点。」

「弗朗西斯，听着，」邦珀斯说。「你是这屋里唯一可能的未来为人父者。本特利的两个儿子可都在这儿。」

===Original===
Mr. Merry faintly sighed, as if he had indeed been working merely from habit; and Mr. Bentley looked up, as though he could see no occasion for himself to speak.

“It must be so nice to have a houseful of boys,” said Miss Lydia. “Boys and men are my province. Now, your woman is a complex creature. I don’t seem to get any hold upon her. It is just meant to show us, that we are all meant for different parts of what has to be done.”

“I gather, Miss Basden, that I am not on what you would call the side of the chosen,” said Francis, with his careful laugh. “I plead guilty to disagreeing with you on the woman’s suffrage question.”

“I think that these changes in the divorce laws will do a great deal towards equalizing the position of women,” said Miss Basden, with terseness and rising colour.

“Miss Basden,” said Francis, after a startled pause, “I should think any man unworthy of the name, who did not feel the old laws a crying disgrace to a civilized country; as you are brave enough to face these subjects. But I confess I should sympathize with you, if you preferred to turn your eyes from them.”

===Chinese===
梅里先生轻轻叹了口气，仿佛他方才果然只是照习惯行事；本特利先生抬起眼来，好像看不出有什么轮到他说话的必要。

「满屋子都是男孩子，一定很好，」莉迪亚小姐说。「男孩和男人是我的领地。至于女人嘛，是复杂的造物。我似乎抓不住她。这正是要让我们明白：该做的事各有各的份，我们各领一份。」

「我听出来了，巴斯登小姐，我不在您所说的蒙拣选的那一边，」弗朗西斯说着，谨慎地笑了一声。「我认罪：在妇女选举权问题上，我与您意见相左。」

「我认为离婚法的这些修订，将大大促进妇女地位的平等，」巴斯登小姐说，语气干脆，脸色涨起来。

「巴斯登小姐，」愕然停顿片刻后，弗朗西斯说，「谁若不觉得旧法律是文明国家的奇耻大辱，我便认为他不配男人这个名称；您有勇气直面这些题目。不过我承认，您若宁可视而不见，我也会同情您。」

===Original===
“Well, I should not sympathize with myself. I don’t think that, because people are safe from the married dangers, they should turn their eyes from others who are not.”

“You will all have your patience tried, if you go on,” said Herrick.

“But I expect they would all keep it,” said Delia, implying that this was a grave necessity.

“I do not know that, Miss Bentley,” said Francis. “I am afraid I must confess that I yield rather easily to impatience.”

“Well, well, it is the same thing,” said Herrick. “The one is a condensed form of the other. Patience contains more impatience than anything else, as I judge.”

===Chinese===
「我可不同情我自己。我不认为，人们自己既然躲开了婚姻的风险，就该对没躲开的人掉转目光。」

「你们再这样谈下去，耐心都要受考验了，」赫里克说。

「可我想他们都顶得住，」迪莉娅说，暗示这是严重的必需。

「这个我可不敢说，本特利小姐，」弗朗西斯说。「恐怕我得承认，我相当容易屈服于急躁。」

「唉，唉，本是一回事，」赫里克说。「一个是另一个的浓缩形态。据我判断，耐心所含的急躁，比什么都多。」

===Original===
“You judge well,” said Bumpus.

“How profound you are, Nicholas!” said Emily. “I have always thought that. Though I have never known that I thought it. Think how it is with everything; how tolerance, for example, is only condensed intolerance, and how it holds more intolerance than anything else. It is just a case for intolerance to be kept in. And think how religion holds more dislike of religion than anything else!”

“Now, Uncle Peter, put them right,” said Francis.

“Well, well, I think myself,” said Mr. Fletcher, “that the old, simple views are the right ones; that patience is as far as can be from impatience, tolerance from intolerance, in a word, good from bad. I think we all think that.”

“Yes, I think we do,” said Delia, gravely.

===Chinese===
「您判断得好，」邦珀斯说。

「你多么深刻，尼古拉斯！」艾米丽说。「我一向这么想。虽说从不知道自己想过。想想万事都是如此；比如宽容，不过是浓缩的不宽容，它盛着的不宽容比什么都多。不宽容正该盛在里面。再想想宗教，它盛着的对宗教的厌恶，也比什么都多！」

「好了，彼得伯伯，把他们的说法纠正过来，」弗朗西斯说。

「唉，唉，我自己认为，」弗莱彻先生说，「古老而简单的看法才是对的；耐心离急躁有十万八千里，宽容离不宽容亦然，一句话，善离恶亦然。我想我们都是这么想的。」

「是啊，我想我们是，」迪莉娅正色说。

===Original===
“I do not think so,” said Emily. “I think that good is bad condensed. I think so, because my brother has told me so. I think a woman ought to think what the men of her family think. You think that too, do you not, Francis? It is right of me to think that good is bad condensed, and holds more bad than anything else, when my brother thinks so, isn’t it?”

“Ah! Miss Herrick,” said Francis, slowly shaking his head.

“Mother, you are not eating,” said Mr. Merry.

“Look, what is that?” said Herrick, hurriedly, pointing to a brooch which Miss Lydia wore. “A most curious thing! A most beautiful piece of old work! Let me see it. Be so very kind to me as to take it off, and pass it to me. Yes; a most exquisite thing of art, a possession for whoever owns it.”

“Oh, my brooch! Yes, I am very fond of my brooch. My dear brooch, that was given me by my sailor-man, who came back to me, so shy and awkward, dear, nice thing, to tell me that he had thought of me! Oh, I would not part with my brooch.”

===Chinese===
「我不这么想，」艾米丽说。「我认为善是浓缩的恶。我这么想，是因为我哥哥这么告诉过我。我认为女人就该想她家里男人所想的。你也这么看，是不是，弗朗西斯？我哥哥这么想的时候，我也想善是浓缩的恶、盛着的恶比什么都多，这是对的，是不是？」

「啊！赫里克小姐，」弗朗西斯缓缓摇着头说。

「妈妈，您没吃东西，」梅里先生说。

「看，那是什么？」赫里克连忙说，指着莉迪亚小姐佩的胸针。「多稀奇的东西！多美的一件古工！让我看看。行行好，把它取下来递给我。是了；一件精妙绝伦的艺品，谁拥有它就是谁的宝。」

「哦，我的胸针！是啊，我顶喜欢我的胸针。我亲爱的胸针，是我的水手送的，他回到我身边，那么害羞，那么局促，亲爱的好人，来告诉我他惦记过我！哦，我的胸针我决不离身。」

===Original===
“How beautiful it must be to have sailor-men and brooches, and not feel that one’s deathbed must be so remorseful!” said Emily.

“Ah, they are my children,” said Miss Lydia.

“You have no children, have you, Mr. Fletcher?” said Delia, smiling.

“Not living,” said Mr. Fletcher. “We have had two sons.”

Theresa gazed fiercely in front of her.

===Chinese===
「有水手、有胸针，临终榻上不必满怀悔恨，这该多美！」艾米丽说。

「啊，他们就是我的孩子，」莉迪亚小姐说。

「您没有孩子，是吧，弗莱彻先生？」迪莉娅微笑着说。

「没有在世的，」弗莱彻先生说。「我们有过两个儿子。」

特蕾莎目光凌厉地望着前方。

===Original===
“Yes, there is not much good in rearing up children, when they are to be killed off one by one.”

“It is the valuable lives that must be used,” murmured Miss Lydia. “That is why they are so precious. Ah! how precious they were!”

Mr. Fletcher looked at his women simply with solicitude. He had no thought that strangers might not have the knowledge of them, that a lifetime had given himself.

“Mrs. Fletcher is very sensitive, is she not?” said Delia to Francis.

“Yes, yes, she is,” said Francis. “She is the most sensitive creature. My uncle has had great trouble with her; to save her all that she could be saved, I mean.” Francis hastened to contradict what might be read into his words. “It is a great responsibility to marry a good woman, and find that she is so wrought upon by things.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，养孩子没什么好处，养大了又让他们一个个给弄死。」

「必得用掉的，正是宝贵的生命，」莉迪亚小姐喃喃道。「所以才那么珍贵。啊！他们多珍贵啊！」

弗莱彻先生只是忧虑地望着他的女眷们。他没想到，旁人未必有他一辈子积下的对她们的了解。

「弗莱彻太太非常敏感，是不是？」迪莉娅对弗朗西斯说。

「是啊，是啊，她是的，」弗朗西斯说。「她是顶顶敏感的人。我伯伯为她受了大苦；为她挡下了能挡的一切，我是这个意思。」弗朗西斯忙不迭地补正话里可能被读出的意思。「娶了位好女人，却发现她如此为外物所动，这责任可不小。」

===Original===
“But she has been the very wife for him, I should think?” said Delia.

“Miss Bentley,” said Francis, leaning forward, “she has been an angel to him.”

“Rather a substantial angel,” said Theresa.

“If that quality, Aunt Theresa, is not possible in angels, I am afraid that we can none of us besides Uncle Peter claim to have anything angelic about us,” said Francis.

“Well, well, if we are as well as may be, as men and women, that is enough,” said Mr. Fletcher, implying that certain comparisons were not of a kind to be made.

===Chinese===
「可我想，她正是配他的妻子？」迪莉娅说。

「本特利小姐，」弗朗西斯欠身向前说，「她于他一直是一位天使。」

「一位分量不小的天使，」特蕾莎说。

「婶婶，倘若天使身上不可能有那种品性，那恐怕除彼得伯伯之外，我们谁也不敢说自己身上有半分天使气了，」弗朗西斯说。

「唉，唉，我们身为男人女人，能怎样康健就怎样康健，也就够了，」弗莱彻先生说，暗示某些比较不属可作之类。

===Original===
“Uncle Peter,” said Francis, “I thank you for your rebuke. One is so prone to get into the way of using lightly words that are to be used in a different spirit. Any check on that is very wholesome, and to me, very welcome.”

“I should not have thought that so many perfect people would be allowed to live together in one house,” said Bumpus.

“Yes, we have been allowed to live together for a long time,” said Miss Lydia, her voice dwelling on the agency. “It has been a happy, happy time. But the end of all good things will come. We must look for it. It is that, that makes them so good. But we shall go on being happy. We have all so much work to do.”

“We shall go on being happy, too,” said Miss Basden. “We have plenty of work to do here as well.”

“Yes, you have, Miss Basden,” said Mr. Merry.

===Chinese===
「彼得伯伯，」弗朗西斯说，「谢谢您的规诫。人太容易养成习惯，轻慢地使用那些原该以另一种心境使用的字眼。对此的任何遏制都大有裨益，于我更是求之不得。」

「我原想不到，这么多完人竟获准同住一个屋檐下，」邦珀斯说。

「是啊，我们获准同住好久了，」莉迪亚小姐说，话音在『获准』上流连。「那是一段快活又快活的时光。可一切好日子都有尽头。我们得盼着它。正是这个，才使它们这么好。不过我们还会快活下去。我们都有那么多事要做。」

「我们也会快活下去的，」巴斯登小姐说。「我们这儿的事也多得做不完。」

「是啊，您有，巴斯登小姐，」梅里先生说。

===Original===
“Miss Basden, it is a thing to be so thankful for,” said Francis, his tone correcting possible regret and shame in Miss Basden, in earning her bread.

“Yes, yes, of course,” said Herrick. “We all feel that in this house, I am sure. Why, I work from early morning to late at night. And I never take a holiday. I can’t see what people want with holidays myself.”

“What do you say to that, Mr. Burgess?” said Mr. Merry.

“Well, I was expecting that, Mr. Merry. We have discussed that question, and understand each other now on it, I think.”

Mr. Burgess opened the door for the women.

===Chinese===
「巴斯登小姐，这是该多么感恩的事，」弗朗西斯说，话音纠正着巴斯登小姐自食其力中可能含有的悔恨与羞愧。

「是啊，是啊，当然，」赫里克说。「这屋里人人都有此感，我敢说。像我，从清早干到深夜。还从不休假。我自己就不明白人们要休假做什么。」

「这个您怎么说，伯吉斯先生？」梅里先生说。

「唉，我就等着这句呢，梅里先生。那个问题我们讨论过了，如今彼此已经了解，我想。」

伯吉斯先生为女眷们开了门。

===Original===
“Mr. Burgess is a beautiful advertisement,” said Emily, as they reached the study. “He shows almost an arrogance. Fancy having that for boys!”

“He has his points,” said Miss Basden.

“You are a martyr, Miss Basden,” said Emily. “The worst of a beautiful advertisement is, that it does need that.”

“Let us talk quickly about servants,” said Mrs. Merry, smiling, “before the men come in.”

“It must be so dreadful to be a servant,” said Emily, “and do the important work of the world. That sort of work, so ill paid and degrading.”

===Chinese===
「伯吉斯先生是一则漂亮的活广告，」走到书房时，艾米丽说。「他简直显出一种傲气。想想孩子们有这么一位！」

「他有他的长处，」巴斯登小姐说。

「您是位殉道者，巴斯登小姐，」艾米丽说。「漂亮的活广告最糟的地方，就在于非得如此。」

「趁男人们还没进来，赶紧说说仆人的事，」梅里太太微笑着说。

「做仆人一定可怕极了，」艾米丽说，「干着世上要紧的活计。那种活计，工钱那么薄，又那么低贱。」

===Original===
“Well, well, it depends upon others. And women vary so, vary so,” said Miss Lydia.

“Well, I do not really think it would be such a bad life,” said Delia. “If I belonged to the working class, I would rather be a servant than many other things.”

“And yet you don’t think it is a bad life,” said Emily; “putting it like that! Fancy having to be of nice appearance; and quick and willing and trustworthy; and not wear spectacles waiting at table, as if one’s sight would alter then!” Emily put up her glass. “It must be very bad to be all that, or anything except the first. And I have never met people of that quality, except Peter and William. And they are neither of them quick. And I am not sure of Peter’s appearance.”

Theresa laughed.

“Well, now, Mr. Herrick, we have all been remiss in not asking you when your book is coming out,” said Francis, as the men came in. “A novel? Is that what they call it? But when is your book coming out, if I may put it in a safe way?”

===Chinese===
「唉，唉，这得看碰上什么人。女人又是千差万别，千差万别，」莉迪亚小姐说。

「唉，我还真不觉得那日子有多坏，」迪莉娅说。「我要是出身劳动人家，比起许多别的营生，倒宁愿做仆人。」

「你还说这不坏——话都让你这么说了！」艾米丽说。「想想吧：得仪表可人；还得手脚麻利、殷勤、可靠；侍候上菜还不许戴眼镜，仿佛一上菜视力就会变样似的！」艾米丽举起了眼镜。「要占全这些，或占全头一条之外的任何一条，日子准坏透了。这种品质的人，我只见过彼得和威廉。他们俩手脚都不麻利。彼得的仪表我也拿不准。」

特蕾莎笑了。

「对了，赫里克先生，我们都失礼了，竟没问问您的书几时问世，」男人们进来时，弗朗西斯说。「一部小说？他们是这么叫它的吧？不过您的书到底几时问世，我能挑个稳妥的问法问问吗？」

===Original===
“Oh, well, I believe very likely not at all. Perhaps I have come to your implied view of it,” said Herrick.

“Oh, well, I shouldn’t perhaps have put it in the way I did. And what does my view have to do with it? But I will confess that it did strike me as not a quite expected thing, that you should give your time to writing a book, that any lady might have written. I don’t mean that a lady could write your book. You won’t take me to mean what I don’t mean?”

“Mr. Fletcher, you are not blind, surely, to the fact that women often equal and surpass men in literary achievement?” said Miss Basden.

“Well, Miss Basden, I plead guilty to being old-fashioned in these matters. It is my inclination to put women on a plane of their own, and to regard them as coming down from it, when they take upon themselves the things that have been held fitter for men. And that perhaps leads to my implying that they do not do so well in those things. But I was meaning quite the opposite of disrespect to them, I assure you.”

“Oh, I see. There is the usual kind of contempt in that sentimental exaltation of women.”

===Chinese===
「哦，这个，我看很可能根本不出了。也许我已经转到了您话里暗含的看法，」赫里克说。

「哦，这个，我或许不该那么说话。我的看法又算得了什么？不过我承认，我当时确实觉得意外：您竟把时间用来写一本随便哪位女士都写得出的书。我不是说哪位女士写得出一部您的书。您不会把我没那个意思的话当成那个意思吧？」

「弗莱彻先生，您总不至于看不见，女性在文学成就上常常比肩并超过男性吧？」巴斯登小姐说。

「唉，巴斯登小姐，在这些事上守旧，我认罪。我生性愿意把女性安置在一个属于她们自己的层面上，而当她们把历来视为更宜于男子的事务揽到自己身上时，我便看作是从那层面下来了。这或许就导致我话里暗含她们做那些事做得不那么好。可我向您保证，我对她们绝无半点不敬，恰恰相反。」

「哦，我明白了。那种对女性的滥情式抬高里，藏着的老一套的轻蔑。」

===Original===
“Miss Basden, believe me, it never has been so with me,” said Francis, earnestly.

“If Francis should marry Miss Basden,” said Emily to Theresa, “the school would go down just as Nicholas begins to need the comforts of old age. Unless he would live here with her.”

“He is good at living in other people’s houses,” said Theresa.

“I suppose living with him would embitter one,” said Emily.

“Suppose Merry should be embittered!” said Bumpus. “His personality would go to pieces.”

===Chinese===
「巴斯登小姐，请相信我，在我这里从来不是那样，」弗朗西斯恳切地说。

「弗朗西斯要是娶了巴斯登小姐，」艾米丽对特蕾莎说，「学校就会走下坡，偏偏赶在尼古拉斯开始需要晚年的安适的时候。除非他肯搬来跟她同住。」

「他惯会住别人的房子，」特蕾莎说。

「我看跟他同住会让人心里发苦，」艾米丽说。

「想想梅里要是心里发苦！」邦珀斯说。「他那个人格就散架了。」

===Original===
“Miss Basden apologizes for being a spinster rather more often than is necessary,” said Emily. “Of course I don’t mean it is not necessary, up to a point.”

“And his marrying her would put a stop to her being self-supporting,” said Bumpus. “That is what he does not like about her.”

“Where is my wife?” said Mr. Merry.

“She has run away and deserted us,” said Delia.

“She has gone up to sit for a few minutes in her room, I think, Mr. Merry,” said Miss Basden, stooping over the fire.

===Chinese===
「巴斯登小姐为独身致歉的次数，未免多于必要，」艾米丽说。「当然我不是说没有必要——在一定限度内。」

「再说他娶了她，她的自食其力就到头了，」邦珀斯说。「这正是他在她身上不喜欢的一点。」

「我妻子呢？」梅里先生说。

「她跑了，把我们撇下不管了，」迪莉娅说。

「我想她上楼去自己屋里坐几分钟，梅里先生，」巴斯登小姐俯身向着炉火说。

===Original===
“Is she not well?” said Herrick, loudly. “Allow me, allow me, Miss Basden.”

“Yes, I think so, Mr. Herrick,” said Miss Basden. “She just wanted a few minutes to herself, as she does sometimes.”

“Not when she has guests!” said Herrick, still rather loudly.

“Hers is a solitary nature, I suppose,” said Miss Lydia, looking up.

“I think we owe this coffee to her,” said Masson, as a tray was brought in, followed by Mrs. Merry.

===Chinese===
「她不舒服吗？」赫里克大声说。「让我来，让我来，巴斯登小姐。」

「是的，我想是，赫里克先生，」巴斯登小姐说。「她只是想独自待几分钟，她有时这样。」

「有客人的时候可不能这样！」赫里克说，声音还是不小。

「她生性好独处吧，我想是，」莉迪亚小姐抬起眼说。

「我想这杯咖啡该归功于她，」马森说，这时托盘端了进来，梅里太太随后也到了。

===Original===
“William, what a charming first word of the evening for you to speak!” said Bumpus. “Giving someone her due.”

“We are very grateful to her for it,” said Miss Basden. “Coffee is a thing I never take on myself. I plead guilty to managing it much less well than Mrs. Merry.”

“I should like there to be no such thing as food, myself,” said Mrs. Merry, leaning back in her chair.

“Yes, Mother,” said Mr. Merry.

Francis looked at Mrs. Merry with long and almost inquiring sympathy.

===Chinese===
「威廉，你今晚开腔的头一句话多么迷人！」邦珀斯说。「还人应得之份。」

「我们为此非常感激她，」巴斯登小姐说。「咖啡这东西我从不接手。我认罪：张罗得远不如梅里太太好。」

「要依我，巴不得世上没有吃食这回事，」梅里太太说着，靠进椅背里。

「是啊，妈妈，」梅里先生说。

弗朗西斯望着梅里太太，带着长久而近乎探询的同情。

===Original===
“We must not ask our friends to dinner again, must we, Merry?” said Herrick in a low tone, breathing deeply.

“Oh, Mr. Herrick!” said Mr. Merry, leaning towards him. “Oh, you are not right, you know. Not if you take us up seriously, just when we are just feeling a little for you, in this little disturbance. No, no, not a disturbance, a pleasure for us all. Yes, yes, we all know.”

“I never can remember to eat, myself,” said Miss Basden, in a tone that addressed the company. “If I can remember, I always do, because I think one can do so much more, if one eats.”

“Ah, Miss Basden,” said Mr. Merry, his voice somehow dying away.

“Ah, the pleasures of our bodies!” said Miss Lydia, with the last word lower. “They are given to us as things that are right for us. If they are used rightly. If they are used rightly.”

===Chinese===
「我们不能再请朋友来吃饭了，是不是，梅里？」赫里克低声说，深深吸着气。

「哦，赫里克先生！」梅里先生朝他俯过身去。「哦，您这就不是了，您知道的。您万不可当真见怪，偏偏在这么一场小乱子里、我们正有点心疼您的时候。不，不，不是乱子，是我们大家的乐事。是啊，是啊，我们都知道。」

「我自己总是想不起来吃东西，」巴斯登小姐用一种面向众人的声调说。「想得起来我总归吃的，因为我觉得，吃了能多做成许多事。」

「啊，巴斯登小姐，」梅里先生说，声音不知怎的低了下去。

「啊，我们身体的快乐！」莉迪亚小姐说，末几个字低了下去。「它们赐给我们，作为于我们相宜之物。只要用得正当。只要用得正当。」

===Original===
“Charles, have you finished your coffee, dear?” said Mrs. Merry.

“Charles?” said Masson. “Oh, it is Merry’s Christian name.”

“How simple and kindly of him it seems to have one!” said Emily.

“A king’s name, too,” said Bumpus.

“Mrs. Merry, have you a name, apart from the maternal one by which we hear you designated?” said Mr. Burgess.

===Chinese===
「查尔斯（Charles），你咖啡喝完了吗，亲爱的？」梅里太太说。

「查尔斯？」马森说。「哦，是梅里的教名。」

「他居然也有一个，显得多么朴实亲切！」艾米丽说。

「还是个国王的名字呢，」邦珀斯说。

「梅里太太，除了我们听人称呼您的那位母亲的身份，您另有名字吗？」伯吉斯先生说。

===Original===
Mr. Merry’s eyes went to Mr. Burgess.

“Yes. My name is Emily,” said Mrs. Merry.

“My third name is Emily,” said Miss Basden. “What a lot of namesakes we are!”

“Yes, Miss Basden; very nice,” said Mr. Merry, his voice just avoiding a note of deprecation.

“And, Miss Basden, if I may ask, what are your other two names?” said Francis.

===Chinese===
梅里先生的眼睛转向伯吉斯先生。

「有。我的名字是艾米丽，」梅里太太说。

「我的第三个名字是艾米丽，」巴斯登小姐说。「我们这么多同名的人！」

「是啊，巴斯登小姐；很好，」梅里先生说，话音险些带出一丝不以为然。

「还有，巴斯登小姐，恕我冒昧，您另外两个名字是什么？」弗朗西斯说。

===Original===
Miss Basden repeated two names; and there was a faint titter from one of the maids who were removing the coffee table.

“Mother,” said Mr. Merry, throwing a fierce glance from habit towards the titter, and rapidly withdrawing it; “what is it you were saying?”

“I hope Mrs. Merry will be quick, before Miss Basden has a fourth name,” said Bumpus. “Francis’ mind is working towards it. She did not say she stopped at three.”

“Oh, didn’t she imply it?” said Emily. “The kitchenmaid, who is second parlourmaid tonight, must find it too much.”

“Isn’t it too much?” said Theresa.

===Chinese===
巴斯登小姐复述了两个名字；收拾咖啡桌的女仆中有一个发出一声轻笑。

「妈妈，」梅里先生说，出于习惯朝那轻笑狠狠投去一瞥，又急忙收回；「您方才要说什么来着？」

「愿梅里太太抓紧，赶在巴斯登小姐生出第四个名字之前，」邦珀斯说。「弗朗西斯的心思正朝那上头转。她可没说她止步于三个。」

「哦，她没有这意思吗？」艾米丽说。「那位今晚兼做二号客厅女仆的厨娘，准觉得受不了。」

「可不是受不了么？」特蕾莎说。

===Original===
“How good we all are at talking without ever saying anything we think!” said Bumpus.

“It is not always politic to say what we think,” said Miss Basden.

“It is not so easy,” said Masson.

“Sometimes I suppose it is right to say it, whether or no we like it, and whether or no it is liked,” said Delia.

“Yes, yes; the thing to be done,” said Miss Lydia, sighing.

===Chinese===
「我们大家多擅长说话啊，说来说去没有一个字是心里所想！」邦珀斯说。

「说出所想并不永远得计，」巴斯登小姐说。

「也并不那么容易，」马森说。

「有时我想，该说的就得说，不管我们爱不爱说，也不管人家爱不爱听，」迪莉娅说。

「是啊，是啊；该做的事，」莉迪亚小姐叹着气说。

===Original===
“Oh, just possibly. Once or twice in a lifetime,” said Mr. Bentley to his daughter.

“Nearer once than twice,” said Bumpus.

“Oh, everyone is not a man,” said Theresa.

“No, that would be a queer state of things,” said Miss Basden.

“Yes,” said Bumpus. “Suppose there had to be two under masters instead of you and Burgess!”

===Chinese===
「哦，也就偶尔可能。一辈子一两回，」本特利先生对女儿说。

「与其说两回，不如说一回，」邦珀斯说。

「哦，并不是人人都是男人，」特蕾莎说。

「是啊，那样世道就怪了，」巴斯登小姐说。

「可不是，」邦珀斯说。「想想吧，要是得用两位副教师来顶你和伯吉斯！」

===Original===
“Oh, Mr. Bumpus!” said Miss Basden.

“Well, would two under mistresses do instead of the two of us?” said Mr. Burgess, not feeling self-suppression a duty in this case. “Or is prejudice the one thing regarded in this school?”

“No, no, Mr. Burgess. Come, you know it is not,” said Mr. Merry. “You are the only sop to it. I mean﻿ ﻿… what I meant was, Mr. Burgess, that if it didn’t matter about having a man on the staff, we should still have been glad to have you, you know. That is what I meant, Mr. Burgess.”

“I meant that, too,” said Bumpus.

“Oh, well, Mr. Merry, you are very kind. But it doesn’t sound as if you would have been much good to me, much compensation to me, in the event of my not being a man.”

===Chinese===
「哦，邦珀斯先生！」巴斯登小姐说。

「那么，两位女副手顶我们两个，行不行？」伯吉斯先生说，在这件事上他不觉得克制自己是义务。「还是说这学校只认偏见一样东西？」

「不，不，伯吉斯先生。得了，您知道不是的，」梅里先生说。「您是给偏见的唯一安抚。我是说……我的意思是，伯吉斯先生，就算教职员里有没有男人无所谓，我们也照样乐意有您，您知道的。这才是我的意思，伯吉斯先生。」

「我也是这个意思，」邦珀斯说。

「哦，好吧，梅里先生，您真是客气。可听上去，万一我不是个男人，您对我未必有多大用处，多大补偿。」

===Original===
Mr. Burgess noted Miss Basden’s expression, and relapsed into peace.

“I should think you are all great readers, Fletcher,” said Herrick. “Miss Fletcher, now, are you not a great person for books?”

“Oh, my books! Yes, I am very fond of my books,” said Miss Lydia. “My dear books, that live in my special case! I love them very much. I am always excited when I have a new companion for them. Yes, I am a great person for my books.”

“Have you a particular kind?” said Herrick.

“Yes,” said Miss Lydia, raising her eyes without lifting her head. “History! Nearly all my books are historical; or if not, biographical. I should think I have not read a novel for thirty years.”

===Chinese===
伯吉斯先生留意到巴斯登小姐的神情，便重归安宁了。

「我看你们都是大读书人，弗莱彻，」赫里克说。「弗莱彻小姐，您呢，您不也是个爱书成癖的人吗？」

「哦，我的书！是啊，我顶喜欢我的书，」莉迪亚小姐说。「我亲爱的书，住在我专用的书柜里！我很爱它们。每添一位新伙伴，我总是兴奋得很。是啊，我是个爱书成癖的人。」

「您有特别偏爱的门类吗？」赫里克说。

「有，」莉迪亚小姐抬起眼睛，头却没有抬。「历史！我的书几乎全是史书；不然就是传记。我估摸着有三十年没读过小说了。」

===Original===
“What books do you read most, Mr. Fletcher?” said Delia, to Francis.

“Miss Bentley, the book I read the most, is, I am thankful to say, the Old Book. My life is largely made up of what must be called the sordid things of the world. But I have the one great privilege.”

“Yes, yes, we can all do with that book. A very good book!” said Herrick.

“Miss Basden, what do you read?” said Emily. “Only the Bible or not?”

“Well, I read a good deal of French, Miss Herrick. I do not know how it is, but I always enjoy books so much more, when they are in French. I feel so much more at home in the language, somehow. Now I very seldom read an English book.”

===Chinese===
「您最常读的是什么书，弗莱彻先生？」迪莉娅问弗朗西斯。

「本特利小姐，我最常读的书，感谢上苍，是那本古书。我的一生大半由世上所谓鄙俗之事构成。但我保有那一样大特权。」

「是啊，是啊，那本书我们谁都受用。一本很好的书！」赫里克说。

「巴斯登小姐，您读什么？」艾米丽说。「只读《圣经》，还是也读别的？」

「这个，我读很多法文书，赫里克小姐。不知怎么，书只要是法文的，我总觉得有趣得多。那种语言，不知怎的让我自在得多。如今英文书我很少读了。」

===Original===
“How wonderful and educational for the boys of you! But what kind of books do you read?”

“Oh, all kinds. I really do not mind, as long as they are French. Or if not French, Italian or German. What I do not like to read, is an English book.”

“Well, Miss Basden, we can very few of us say that,” said Francis.

“I believe I really read the Bible the most, too,” said Delia.

“It is a good thing we did not all live in the time when the Bibles were chained up in the churches,” said Bumpus. “It would have been so lonely.”

===Chinese===
「这对孩子们来说多么美妙、多有教益！可您读的是哪一类书呢？」

「哦，哪类都行。我真不在乎，只要是法文的。法文不行，意大利文或德文也成。我不爱读的，是英文书。」

「唉，巴斯登小姐，我们中间能这么说的人可没几个，」弗朗西斯说。

「我想其实我也最常读《圣经》，」迪莉娅说。

「幸亏我们不全生活在《圣经》还锁在教堂里的时代，」邦珀斯说。「那可太冷清了。」

===Original===
“Well, but it is a good thing to read the Bible,” said Francis.

“Of course it is the most beautiful book,” said Emily. “And now we are modern, and only read about wickedness, it is so nice to have it unchained, isn’t it? It really ought to be chained, I think.”

“Ah, you try to get at us, Miss Herrick,” said Francis, not quite checking a laugh.

“Ah, there is much warning in it for us,” said Miss Lydia.

“In all great pictures,” said Francis, firmly pulling himself together, and finding the effort inspiring; “we are shown the dreadful as well as the beautiful. We are surely not given only one side of the lesson.”

===Chinese===
「唉，可读《圣经》终究是好事，」弗朗西斯说。

「它当然是最美的书，」艾米丽说。「如今我们摩登了，只读恶行，它解了锁真好，是不是？要我说，它真该重新锁上。」

「啊，您这是想拿话套我们，赫里克小姐，」弗朗西斯说，没能完全止住笑。

「啊，书里给我们留着许多警戒呢，」莉迪亚小姐说。

「在一切伟大的图画里，」弗朗西斯说，毅然振作起来，还觉得这番努力令人振奋；「示于我们的既有可怖，也有美。功课总不会只给我们一面。」

===Original===
“I am one of the greatest readers alive,” said Herrick. “I have read all European modern literature, the enormous bulk of it. And I have read as much medieval literature as any man living. And I know my Greek and Latin. They were taught us well when I was a boy.”

“But you need not imply how they are taught now,” said Bumpus.

“What do you read, Mr. Masson?” said Mrs. Merry.

“Very little off my own line, Mrs. Merry. Miss Austen is the novelist I read the most.”

“What do you think of her books, Mr. Fletcher?” said Delia to Francis.

===Chinese===
「我是当世最大的读书人之一，」赫里克说。「欧洲现代文学我读遍了，那浩浩的一大堆。中古文学，我读得不比世上任何人少。我的希腊文和拉丁文也拿得起来。我小时候，这两样教得好。」

「可您不必暗示如今教得怎样，」邦珀斯说。

「您读什么书，马森先生？」梅里太太说。

「本行之外读得很少，梅里太太。奥斯汀小姐是我读得最多的小说家。」

「您觉得她的书怎么样，弗莱彻先生？」迪莉娅问弗朗西斯。

===Original===
“I am afraid, Miss Bentley, that I have very little use for books written by ladies for ladies, if I may so express myself; though I dare say I should be the better for them.”

“Oh, no, you would not. You could not be,” said Bumpus.

“It is the other way round,” said Masson.

“Personally, I can’t get over the littleness in her books,” said Miss Basden.

“Ah, we are not small enough, not small enough,” said Miss Lydia.

===Chinese===
「恕我直言，本特利小姐，女士写给女士看的书，我实在用不上多少；虽说读读想必对我有益。」

「哦，不，不会有益。你不可能更好，」邦珀斯说。

「是反过来的，」马森说。

「就我个人而言，她书里那股小家子气，我怎么也过不去，」巴斯登小姐说。

「啊，我们还不够小，还不够小，」莉迪亚小姐说。

===Original===
“The best goods are done up in a certain way,” said Mr. Fletcher, smiling.

“It is my great regret that Jane Austen died so young,” said Masson.

“And one used to think that forty odd was not so young after all,” said Herrick.

“I never think about my age,” said Miss Basden.

“Ah, but we are in progress,” breathed Miss Lydia, “simply in progress.”

===Chinese===
「上等货有上等货的包法，」弗莱彻先生微笑着说。

「我最大的憾事，是简·奥斯汀（Jane Austen）去世太早，」马森说。

「从前人们还觉得四十出头到底不算年轻呢，」赫里克说。

「我从不想到自己的年纪，」巴斯登小姐说。

「啊，可我们正在进步，」莉迪亚小姐低声说，「单单是在进步。」

===Original===
“To what may be big enough in the end for us,” said Theresa.

“One is really a hero for not committing suicide, I suppose,” said Mr. Burgess.

“Now, Mr. Burgess, none of that sort of talk with the boys,” said Mr. Merry.

“Oh, I very seldom talk to the boys, Mr. Merry.”

“I hope no one has seen me talking to my undergraduates,” said Bumpus.

===Chinese===
「进到头来也许够大的什么，」特蕾莎说。

「想来，不自杀的人实在都算英雄，」伯吉斯先生说。

「好了，伯吉斯先生，跟孩子们可不许说这类话，」梅里先生说。

「哦，我很少跟孩子们说话，梅里先生。」

「但愿没人见过我跟我那些本科生说话，」邦珀斯说。

===Original===
“No one has. Don’t boast, Dickie,” said Emily.

“We must be going,” said Theresa.

“So must we,” said Delia. “We ought to be quite ashamed of staying so long, oughtn’t we, Father?”

“It has been too great a pleasure for us to feel compunction about seeming to appreciate it,” said Mr. Bentley, his voice falling with an unfamiliar sound.

“Thank you for coming and bearing it all,” said Emily. “It has been a great thing for us to have such glimpses of family life. My brother and I are orphans, you know.”

===Chinese===
「没人见过。别吹了，迪基，」艾米丽说。

「我们该走了，」特蕾莎说。

「我们也该走了，」迪莉娅说。「待这么久，我们真该臊得慌，是不是，爸爸？」

「这乐趣太大，我们顾不上为了显得领情而愧疚了，」本特利先生说，嗓音低落下来，带着一种陌生的声调。

「多谢二位光临，还忍受了这一切，」艾米丽说。「能这样窥见家庭生活，对我们是件大事。您知道的，我哥哥和我都是孤儿。」

===Original===
“And I am half an orphan,” said Mr. Burgess. “So it is very suitable all round.”

“Now, look here, Mr. Burgess,” said Mr. Merry, in a low tone, “there is no need for you to be standing about, saying goodbye to everyone, you know. Now suppose you go off somewhere to have a smoke, you know.”

“Goodbye, Mr. Fletcher.” Mr. Merry took upon himself the duties of host. “No more prizes yet for you to give away. The lads like to work for their rewards, bless them. And Mr. Bentley. Ah, the two boys! I won’t tell you now what we think of them, because that must be kept for a day when we have had less pleasure.”

Mr. Merry returned to the sitting-room, rubbing his hands, and looking about him with much fondness, as if using up the residue of the feelings gathered for his guests.

“Well, we shall start off work tomorrow with quite a spurt,” said Miss Basden.

===Chinese===
「我还是半个孤儿，」伯吉斯先生说。「所以各方面都正合适。」

「好了，听我说，伯吉斯先生，」梅里先生低声说，「您犯不着在这儿站着，跟每个人道别，您知道的。这样吧，您找个地方抽烟去，怎么样，您知道的。」

「再见，弗莱彻先生。」梅里先生把主人的职责担了起来。「暂时没有奖要请您发了。孩子们喜欢为奖赏下功夫，愿老天保佑他们。还有本特利先生。啊，那两个孩子！我们现在怎么看待他们，眼下先不告诉您，因为那得留给一个我们没这么高兴的日子说。」

梅里先生回到起居室，搓着双手，深情地环顾四周，仿佛在把为客人们攒下的情感余烬用尽。

「好了，明天我们开工准会来个冲刺，」巴斯登小姐说。

===Original===
“Yes, Miss Basden,” said Mr. Merry. “Not that you are in much need of that.”

“Well, but it is an opportunity for you to get a hit at me, Mr. Merry,” said Mr. Burgess, strolling back into the room.

“Oh, Mr. Burgess! Get a hit at you! Why, you try to misunderstand me, unless you like to be treated as if you were seventy. Good night, Miss Herrick. And Mr. Herrick. Yes, Mr. Burgess. Are you coming up with us? Such a pleasure to us all. Not at all a trouble. I mean, what a pleasure in proportion to the little arranging, you know.”

Mr. Merry hastened after his wife, as if fearing to break his impression.

“Well, I have not much to boast of,” said Herrick, sitting down by the fire. “Seventy years old, and nothing before me, and nothing behind to count! Why, I could almost envy Merry, for being only fifty, and wanting nothing more than he has.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，巴斯登小姐，」梅里先生说。「虽说您也并不怎么需要冲刺。」

「可这正好给您个机会敲打我一下，梅里先生，」伯吉斯先生说着，踱回屋里来。

「哦，伯吉斯先生！敲打您！哎呀，您是非要误会我不成，除非您乐意让人当您七十岁伺候。晚安，赫里克小姐。还有赫里克先生。是了，伯吉斯先生。您跟我们一起上楼吗？我们大家的高兴劲儿就别提了。一点也不麻烦。我是说，比起那么一点点安排，这份高兴多划算，您知道的。」

梅里先生赶忙跟上妻子，仿佛怕打断了自己的余兴。

「唉，我可没什么可夸口的，」赫里克说着，在炉边坐下。「七十岁了，身前无一物，身后无可数！唉，我几乎要羡慕梅里了，他才五十，得到的又不多想要一分。」

===Original===
“You should not talk about ‘almost envying’ Mr. Merry, as if he belonged to people like that,” said Emily. “And I don’t know about his not wanting more than he has. I don’t think he wants that much. And fifty isn’t such a good age as all that. And not much to boast of! With seventy years of safe life behind you, when most people have the risk of having so much less! And all the life in front of you, that people of seventy always have!”

“Yes, yes, Emily. I dare say I shall have a time yet. Time to do something in. It sometimes happens that the end of a man’s life sums him up. There is no great wrong about being an exception. Exceptions are more worthy of interest in a way. I don’t think I have ever been quite on the ordinary line.”

“No, I am sure you have not. It would have been dreadful of you.”

===Chinese===
「你不该说什么『几乎要羡慕』梅里先生，倒像他属于那一类人似的，」艾米丽说。「再说他不要更多的东西，这我也不知道。我看他要的也没那么多。再说五十岁也没好到那个地步。还没什么可夸口的！身后是七十年稳稳当当的日子，大多数人可冒着连这一半都捞不着的险！身前呢，还有七十岁的人从来都有的那一整段人生！」

「是啊，是啊，艾米丽。我看我的时日还有呢。还有做点事情的工夫。人一生的收尾有时恰是他一生的总结。做个例外也没有什么大不了。从某种意义上说，例外倒更值得注意。我想我从来不曾完全走在寻常的线上。」

「是啊，你肯定不曾。你要是走过，那才可怕呢。」
